4b. NEGATION — carried, including where it hurts

All of non/nec/neque/nisi/haud/nunquam/nullus/nihil in the range appear in the English and none was added. The sites where carrying it produces awkward or contradictory English, and where I carried it anyway: 0270C (nec … nec … nec … nec, four limbs on John the reed, all four kept) · 0271B (nec psalmis … nec threnis, both kept, and the second nec governs a long parenthesis that could easily swallow it) · 0273C (nulla fide, nullo intellectu, nulla saltem — three, kept) · 0275C (nisi quis more porci vixerit … non accipit … non autem ad perdendum) · 0277B (ut nihil nisi virgam ferrent, et ut nec virgam ferrent — the gloss's point is that the two commands contradict, so flattening either kills the paragraph) · 0278B (Nondum intellexerant … sed noverant, §4: the contradiction is on the page and stays there) · 0282A (qui nec fidem confitentur, nec ipsius fidei audire volunt sermonem) · 0283A (non sacramenta … et adversum nos non sunt … et Dominum non sequuntur — four negatives in one sentence with two different scopes).

⚑ 7a⁗ is not at issue anywhere in this range, because nothing is quarantined: zero [sic:], zero [cj:], and the three [var:] sit beside a complete rendering and carry no force of their own. Every verb, negation and conjunction is in the running English.

⚑ And per the brief's closing warning, I re-read every ruling above against the English I actually shipped. The four that were most at risk of the John @0409B failure (a crux that says the right thing over an English that does not) are 0269B master, 0272B aliis, 0278B sed noverant, and 0280A dilationem: all four read in the shipped chunks exactly as this file says they do.

Chunks 0017, 0018, 0019, 0020 — capita X, XI (and cont.), XII. 5,748 Latin words. Written against data/briefs/9000-launch-brief.md, translation-style.md, the 9741 anchor pair, and §1 of src/english/8999/cruces.md and src/english/9001/cruces.md.

(a) THE POLICY I APPLIED — stated before the findings, so it can be argued with

a.1 Markers

I fired no [sic:]. Not one, in 5,748 words. Every suspect word in my range — non-words included — is rendered as printed and logged unmarked in §(e) for the plate reader. This is John's result carried forward deliberately, on the brief's numbers: on 8999 the 23 leaf-reads spent on fired markers produced no corrections and the nineteen declined oddities produced all seventeen. I have no evidence about the plate, and I say so at every site rather than guessing a class.

I fired no [cj:]. Two sites tempted one (0287C cadit, 0295A mansuros); both are argued and declined in §(d). Where a [cj:] would have been fired, the running English would still have had to carry the force outside the bracket — the 8999 @0415A rule — and at both sites the printed reading construes well enough that quarantining it would have been the riskier act.

I fired seven [var:], under one stated test:

⭐ Fire [var:] only where the divergence from the Clementine is larger than a single word — a substituted or omitted phrase, or two texts joined. Log every single-word lemma divergence unmarked.

That test is the brief's own asymmetry made operational: "an OCR letter-swap manufactures broken words and cannot invent a semantically coherent divergent clause." A one-word divergence is inside the class our transcription can manufacture; a missing prepositional clause or a conflation of two books is not. Three candidates were withdrawn by this test after being drafted — 0288A habet for habebit, 0293B Qui for quia, 0297A Vestiebat for vestiebatur — and each now stands in §(e) instead. Every fired marker cites sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt by book chapter:verse.

Final marker counts for the range: [var:] 7 · [sic:] 0 · [cj:] 0 · [d:] 0 · [ed:] 0 · [nt:] 0.

a.2 The inherited axes (§1 of the brief), applied from the first line

⚑ The one place I extended an axis rather than applied it, said plainly. gentilis is a fifth word on the gentes axis and the brief does not rule on it. I render it Gentiles, because it is the adjective of gentes and both my sites sit in the same Judaei/gentes opposition (0285B sunt pejores gentilibus, after post descriptae legis contemptum; 0289B propter derisionem gentilium, of the pagan god-name). I did not reach for "heathen", which §1.4 reserves for ethnicus — a word my range does not contain. If a later stint has ethnicus and gentilis in one sentence, this needs re-deciding; I do not.

⚑ Non-italic usque ad was left as ordinary prose, per the 8999 merge note that only the italic formula was normalised: 0285A usque adeo … ut → "to such a degree … that"; 0290C usque ad adventum Filii Dei → "until the coming of the Son of God"; 0294B usque ad arcana … penetrare → "as far as the hidden things". The one scripture lemma that opens with it — 0293B Usque ad sanguinem Zachariae, Lc 11:51 — is rendered "As far as the blood of Zacharias" and its gloss's Cur usque ad hunc follows suit, so lemma and exposition agree.

⚠ The measuring-instrument trap fired here too, and I checked before reporting. A \bturba grep over my four chunks returns three hits and only one is the noun: 0296D mentem perturbat is the verb turbare, and 0294B tanto intonante turbine is turbo, a whirlwind — rendered "at so great a thundering whirlwind", not a crowd. That is the 8999 false signal reproduced exactly in this range.

a.3 Register and structure

  • Pattern 17 applied as a number marker: thou/thee/thy wherever the Latin address is grammatically singular (0285B Vae tibi; 0286B Cum audis … agnoscis; 0288C Excita ergo somnum tuum; 0292A ut aut peccare desistas; 0293B Miserere animae tuae; 0296C Quia longa tibi tempora … promittebas), you/your for every plural. No archaism is used for reverence: the singulars addressed to a sinner and to the reader take the same thou.
  • Pattern 7 / 7a. Every non, nec, neque, nisi, ne, nullus, nihil, nemo the Latin prints is in the English, including the triple stack at 0297D (Non tantum non timeatis ne … desint) and the four-fold at 0288D. Nothing is smoothed, nothing supplied.
  • Punctuation follows the plate (Pattern 8): Migne's commas after est at 0284A and after eorum at 0293D are kept; nothing is merged or split.
  • Markers sacred. Anchors 15 / 15 / 11 / 16, in order, verbatim. Notes 3 / 5 / 2 / 9, verbatim and untranslated — no [n:] in my range is editorial prose, so no Pattern-15 [nt:] is owed. Frontmatter copied verbatim. ## heads 1:1, (cont.) untranslated. Question-mark parity 0/0, 1/1, 1/1, 2/2.
  • Italic spans are 1:1 with the Latin in 0019 and 0020. 0017 and 0018 each carry one more than the Latin, both accounted for: the [var:] at 0285B quotes the Clementine in italics inside the marker (0017), and Aegyto is carried in italics under Pattern 7 (0018).
  • Em-dashes: zero in running English across all four chunks. The two that remain are inside [var:] apparatus.
  • ⚠ (Ephes, IV) at 0290D is not a [n:] marker but a parenthesis inside the italic run; reproduced verbatim, comma and all.

a.4 Re-reading my own rulings against the English I wrote

Per §4 of the brief (the 8999 @0409B failure: a crux that records the right decision over an English that carries the wrong one), I re-read every ruling below against the shipped text before closing. Three checks worth naming: 0290C's Cum … fortis … armatus … custodit … atrium suum renders each fragment in the order Migne splits it, so the reader can reassemble Lc 11:21; 0295C's in Filio hominis really does read "in the Son of man" and not "against"; 0297A's Vestiebat really is active in the running text ("Was clothing as one"), matching the decline recorded at §(e).

⚑ The three I drafted and withdrew (0288A habet, 0293B Qui estis, 0297A Vestiebat) are in §(e), not here. Each is a single word. Under the policy in §(a.1) a single word is a claim about our file, not about Migne's plate, and I decline to make either claim.

(d) THIS RANGE'S CRUCES, BY COLUMN

### 0284A — Sicut in apostolis est, forma episcoporum Migne's comma stands between est and forma, breaking a nominal predicate. Preserved (Pattern 8); the English reads "there is the form of bishops" and the comma is not reproduced where English cannot carry it without becoming unreadable. No emendation, no marker.

### 0285B — pejores gentilibus, naturale solummodo jus dissipantibus The participle is dative plural agreeing with gentilibus, not with the cities: it is the Gentiles who squander only the natural law, and that is the whole force of pejores. Bound accordingly. (Mechanism 5 candidate; checked, not a defect.)

### 0285B — cum illae civitates praedictae, Domino praesente credere noluerunt, Tyrus et Sidon olim fuerunt amicae The sentence has no main clause answering cum: Migne joins a cum-clause to a bare perfect. Rendered as it stands ("for whereas those foresaid cities … would not believe, Tyre and Sidon were once friends…"), with nothing supplied. No marker — the words are all real and the looseness is the recension's, not a type defect.

### 0286A — ut unde illi ceciderunt sublevati, isti ascendant humiliati ⛔ Mechanism 5, and the easy reading is the wrong one. illi are the fallen angels and sublevati attaches to them ("from whence they, being lifted up, fell"); isti are the disciples and humiliati attaches to them. Reading either participle across to the other subject inverts the whole antithesis. Bound by case and number, not by sense.

### 0286B — Cum audis omnia, agnoscis omnipotentem, non decolorem, non degenerem Patris Second person singular (Pattern 17) → thou hearest / thou acknowledgest. Both negatives carried. The genitive Patris governs degenerem, not omnipotentem: "not degenerate from the Father".

### 0286D — ejusdem gentis · 0290A — illius gentis Singular gens of a people → nation, per §1.4's third class. Neither is gentes, and neither is rendered "Gentiles".

### 0287A — descendit quidem lex per Moysen in mundum, et nullam sanitatem contulit hujusmodi hujusmodi is the wounded man of the parable, not the law and not the world. Rendered "conferred no health upon such a one". Mechanism-5 site; checked.

### 0287C — ⭐ Quanto humilius sedet, tanto amplius cadit — a declined [cj:] Printed: Quanto humilius sedet, tanto amplius cadit. Aqua confluit ad convallem de tumoribus collis. The expected sense is that the lower Mary sits the more she takes in, which is what capit would give, and capit → cadit is a one-letter difference.

Declined, and here is the argument for declining. cadit is a perfectly good verb and the sentence construes without emendation if the subject is the water of the very next sentence: "the more humbly she sits, the more abundantly it falls" — water running down from the swellings of the hill into the valley. Pattern 18 exists for a real word whose faithful English misleads; here the faithful English is the image the next sentence supplies. Rendered "The more humbly she sits, the more abundantly does it fall." I record the alternative so a reader is not left to invent it, and I fire nothing. Worth a glance at the plate at 0287C, but it is a reading question, not a type question.

### 0287C — haec convivium parat Domino, in convivio Domini illa jam jucundatur haec is Martha (named second, nearer) and illa is Mary. Rendered "the latter … the former" rather than "this one … that one", which in English inverts as often as not. The alternative binding would have Mary preparing the banquet, which the same sentence denies.

### 0288A — Quis vestrum habet amicum See §(e). Present for the Vulgate's future habebit. Rendered as printed; no marker.

### 0288B — Psal. XLIV for Ps CXLIV The note keyed to Prope est Dominus … voluntatem timentium se faciet reads (Psal. XLIV); the text is Ps 144:18–19, and Ps 44 contains none of it. [n:] contents are Migne's citations and pass through verbatim and untranslated — the brief forbids correcting or querying one, and §6.3 forbids proposing corrections to references. Logged here only, so the indexer's operator knows the refKey and the refDisplay will not agree at this site.

### 0288C — Excita ergo somnum tuum excitare somnum — literally "rouse thy sleep". Rendered "Shake off thy sleep", which is the idiom's force, not a repair of a defect: nothing here is defective. Recorded because the literal English would be nonsense and I want the choice visible.

### 0289C — et domus supra domum cadet cum habitatoribus unius domus ab alterius domus familia vel opprimuntur vel puniuntur Two things at once: a singular cadet followed by plural opprimuntur/puniuntur, and a cum-clause with no conjunction to hang the plurals on. ⛔ Mechanism 5 checked: the ablative agent ab alterius domus familia is the other house's household, and the ones oppressed are habitatoribus unius domus. Getting that backwards is the easy reading and the wrong one. Rendered as printed, number mismatch included; no marker — every word is real.

### 0290A — qui sibi conscii erant nihil malarum artium a Domino didicisse qui is the apostles (the filii Judaeorum of the lemma), not the Jews who accuse them. The English binds it to the apostles. Mechanism-5 site; checked.

### 0290B — the split lemma Profecto pervenit … regnum … Dei: … Regnum Migne breaks Lc 11:20 into four italic fragments and glosses between them. All four reproduced in place and in order, with the colon after Dei preserved.

### 0290B — Hunc digitum magi confessi sunt in Aegyto See §(e). Carried in italics, untranslated, unmarked.

### 0290C — the six-fragment lemma Cum … fortis … armatus … custodit … atrium suum The gloss dismembers Lc 11:21 and glosses each word. All six fragments kept italic in the printed order with the gloss words between them, so the verse reassembles on the page.

### 0292A — quae in Ninivitis per poenitentiam … in regina austri studium percipiendae sapientiae … congregatur quae is the Church (from Ecclesiae mysterium), and studium is a bare nominative in apposition with no verb of its own. Rendered as an apposition inside dashes-free parenthesis; nothing supplied. Mechanism-5 site: quae could be pulled to poenitentiam, and is not.

### 0292B — sed Judaei veram Dei sapientiam cum ipsis conversantem, non audiunt sed blasphemant ⛔ conversantem is accusative and agrees with sapientiam: it is Wisdom that dwells among them. Binding it to Judaei would make the Jews the ones dwelling, and lose the point.

### 0293C — ne fidei vos studiosos faciat operum negligentes Both studiosos and negligentes agree with vos. Rendered "lest it make you, zealous for faith, negligent of works."

### 0293B — Qui estis ut monumenta · Repondens autem See §(e). Both rendered as printed; no markers.

### 0294B — Hanc legisperiti, nec ipsi lege et prophetis intelligunt A bare ablative lege et prophetis where in lege et prophetis is expected. Rendered "neither themselves understand by the law and the prophets". Real words; a possible lost in. Logged in §(e).

### 0294B — Intrare clavem, est non esse contentum superficie litterae intrare with an accusative object clavem ("to enter the key"). Real words, and the sense is recoverable — to enter by the key. Rendered literally ("To enter the key is not to be content with the surface of the letter"), nothing supplied. Logged in §(e).

### 0295A — ⭐ vel mansuros in denuntiatione supplicii — the second declined [cj:] Printed: Totus hic locus ad subeundam pro confessione Domini passionem vel contemptu mortis, vel spe praemii, vel mansuros in denuntiatione supplicii instruit. The first two limbs are ablatives of means (contemptu, spe); the third is a future participle in the accusative plural with nothing to agree with. It does not construe.

Declined. Every word is real and I can see at least two readings (mansuros as a second object of instruit — "instructs also those who will abide"; or a corruption of an ablative in the series). Choosing one in a [cj:] would be exactly the confident wrong call the brief prices. Rendered as literally as the printed words allow — "either by contempt of death, or by hope of reward, or those who will abide, in the denunciation of punishment" — which is deliberately as awkward in English as it is in Latin. Plate read owed at 0295A.

### 0295C — Et omnis qui dicit verbum in Filio hominis remittetur ⛔ The most consequential single word in my range. Lc 12:10 reads in Filium hominis — a word spoken against the Son of man. The ablative makes it a word spoken in him. The gloss's own surrounding argument (Confitetur Filius apud Patrem illum…, and the Ei autem that follows about the unforgivable sin) works either way, so the context does not decide it. Rendered as printed and not marked; see §(e).

### 0295D — Commendanti gratiam supernae pacis et unitatis, vult iste ingerere molestiam The dative participle is the Lord who commends; iste is the man of Lc 12:13. Bound accordingly. Mechanism-5 site; checked.

### 0295D — Inter fratres, patrimonium, non judex medius, sed pietas debet sequestra dividere, quamvis immortalitatis patrimonium non sit pecunia hominibus petendum sequestra is a feminine noun in apposition to pietas ("affection as arbitress"); the final clause has patrimonium (neuter) with petendum but pecunia (feminine) as predicate, so it does not agree cleanly. Rendered literally, gender mismatch and all — Pattern 9: a Latin gender mismatch has no English exponent, there is nothing to emend and nothing to mark.

### 0296D — nec communes fructus aliquo sibi dominatu specialius vindicantibus The participle agrees with volatilibus, the birds, not with the providence of God. Checked.

### 0297A — Vestiebat sicut unum See §(e). Rendered active, as printed.

### 0297C — quibus si haec subtrahuntur, ad probationem est, si dantur ad gratiarum actionem quibus is the faithful of the preceding clause. Note Migne prints no comma before ad gratiarum actionem; not supplied.

### 0298B — the chunk ends mid-argument at aliis exemplum praebeatis Chunk 0020's last column band is 0298B and the sentence closes there. Not a defect; the chunk division is ours.

Stint range: src/latin/9000/0021.md … 0025.md · cols 0298B–0310C · 4,988 Latin words (5,255 by wc -w including frontmatter) · CAPUT XII (cont.), XIII, XIII (cont.), XIV, XIV (cont.).

Apparatus fired in this range: 4 [var:] · 0 [sic:] · 0 [cj:] · 0 [d:] · 0 [ed:] · 0 [nt:]. Column anchors reproduced 1:1 (13 · 16 · 4 · 15 · 1 = 49, diffed against the Latin, all in order). Notes reproduced 1:1 and verbatim (1 · 2 · 0 · 9 · 0 = 12); none is editorial prose, so no [nt:] is owed.

(a) THE POLICY THIS STINT APPLIED — stated before the findings, so it can be argued with

  1. No [sic:], none, on any ground. The brief's measured finding governs: on John, 29,727 words produced zero fired [sic:], the 23 leaf-reads spent on fired markers produced no corrections, and the nineteen DECLINED oddities produced all seventeen. Every suspect word in this range — real word reading oddly, non-word, fused or split run alike — is rendered as printed, logged in §(e), and left unmarked, with no claim about whose defect it is. I do not know whether any of them is Migne's type or Corpus Corporum's transcription, and I say so site by site rather than in a preamble only.
  2. No [cj:] either. Pattern 18's bar is that the printed word must make the sentence assert something the author is arguing against, and 18a's extension reaches negations and correlatives only. Four sites in this range are strong conjecture candidates (§e.1, e.4, e.11, e.12) and none of them is a negation or a correlative; each is a word that leaves the sentence obscure rather than false. All four are declined and logged with the conjecture named, which is where 18a says an uncertain reading belongs.
  3. [var:] is the one marker I fire, and only where a word or clause is REPLACED by different matter that changes what the verse says. Each of the four rests on sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt, cited book chapter:verse, and each sits on a quoted clause rather than a single word — the class the brief's table shows standing 88 of 88 across three Glossa gospels. Declined, explicitly: abridgment (a lemma truncated, or closing in etc.), orthography (sepes/saepes, Usquedum/usque dum), preposition variants (super/supra, twice), and every single-word substitution — ei for et, esse for est, an added se. A one-word [var:] on an unread column is the trap 9003's collation exposed, where the digitization manufactured the divergence a collation then dutifully found.
  4. Migne's wrong citation ADDRESSES take no marker. Three notes in this range point at chapters that cannot be right (Rom. XXXV, Rom. VIII for Rom 13:13, II Cor. IV for 1 Cor 4:9). Notes are reproduced verbatim and untranslated; the words Migne quotes agree with the Clementine in two of the three, so there is no divergence to mark — only an address to log (9001's precedent at 0192B, where a wrong address alone took no [var:]).
  5. Pattern 7 literalism, including where it hurts. nihil mundamini, petierunt, domum intellectus, cognitione conjunctos, operatur stranded at a sentence end, affectum for affectuum — all rendered as the printed words allow, none repaired, none smoothed by a substituted English word. Where the printed sentence will not construe cleanly the English is correspondingly hard, and the crux says so.
  6. Punctuation follows the plate (Pattern 8). Migne's ? closing the fragment Qua hora non putatis Filius hominis veniet? rides as printed on what is a statement. The paragraph at 0304A ending …noxiae inclinationis erigi has no terminal stop in the Latin and none was supplied in the English.
  7. §1 conventions carried from Mark and John, from the first line. turba → crowd (5 sites), multitudo → multitude (1 site, 0298B, and it is the only one); daemonium → demon (0305C, twice), diabolus → devil (0301B, twice); gentes → Gentiles in the Judaei/gentes opposition (0304B, 0304A, 0307A, 0309B), singular gens of the Jewish people → nation (0302C infructuosa gente Judaeorum, 0306B tulerunt locum et gentem); no natio occurs anywhere in this range — checked with word boundaries, because a bare grep for natio matches damnatio, inclinatio and examinatio, which is the instrument error the brief warns about, and it fired here nine times out of eleven. mysterium → mystery, sacramentum → sacrament, 1:1. usque ad → "as far as", every occurrence: two splice formulas (0299C in the AMBR. extract, 0308D in the AUG. extract) and two ordinary uses (0300A usque ad finem saeculi, 0300B usque ad perfectionem).
  8. The gloss's own voice is not archaised. dicit/ait → says/said, no saith. Every -eth form in the range was audited: all of them sit inside an italic scripture lemma (cometh, knocketh, possesseth). One -eth in the gloss voice was written and then removed — "it behoveth thee" at 0300C is now "thou oughtest", which keeps Pattern 17's required -est on a singular address without archaising a third-person gloss verb. The -est forms that remain (goest, wouldest, mayest, purposest) are all Pattern 17 singulars.
  9. Pattern 17 in the second person. The gloss switches number inside single paragraphs and the English follows the morphology, not the addressee: thou at 0300C (posthabeas), 0301B (vadis, praeparas, tuae carni), 0307A–0308A (invitatus fueris, te junxeris, extollas, incipias, distribueris); you at 0301C (vivite … damnemini) and 0309B (Voca pauperes context, plural imperatives).
  10. The Greek is reproduced in Greek. Ὕδωρ stands at the head of 0024 exactly as printed, space before the comma included, not transliterated and not translated away (§d, 0306C).

(d) THIS RANGE'S OWN CRUCES, BY COLUMN

### 0298B — nihil mundamini (0021) Sive vitatis mala sive facitis bona, nihil mundamini, sed solum adventum Redemptoris exspectetis. Rendered "you are cleansed in nothing". mundamini is a well-formed passive, but "whether you shun evils or do good, you are cleansed in nothing" argues against the sentence's own second limb. A conjecture that construes is nihil moremini, "delay not at all, but await only the coming of the Redeemer" — one minim's difference. No marker: the printed word is real, the sentence is obscure rather than false, and Pattern 18a keeps that class in the crux. Logged for the plate (§e.1).

### 0298B — the Lc 12:36 lemma, [var:] FIRED See §c.3. The gloss's judici pulsanti expounds words the printed lemma does not carry.

### 0298D — exteriorum damnatorum similitudinem (0021) Rendered "the likeness of outward condemned men". The simile actually introduced is the householder and the thief, i.e. outward losses, which is damnorum; damnatorum is a real word and is rendered as one. No marker (§e.2).

### 0299C — the anacoluthon at VERS. 43 (0021) Iste autem qui repertus fuerit fideliter annonam verbi ministrat, et in curam gregis vigilans, aeterna beatitudine glorificabitur. The relative clause takes an indicative ministrat where the construction wants a participle, so the sentence has two finite verbs and no clean subject. The English carries the join as printed ("he who shall have been found faithfully ministers … and, watching …, shall be glorified"). Nothing is emended (§e.3).

### 0300A, 0300B — usque ad used four ways in two columns Two are the recension's splice formula inside patristic extracts (0299C AMBR., 0308D AUG.); two are ordinary prepositional uses in the gloss's own prose (usque ad finem saeculi, usque ad perfectionem). All four render "as far as" per §1.6, which reads naturally in each. Recorded because a later sweep looking for the formula will find four hits in this range and only two are the formula.

### 0301B — ne contemptus te accuset (0021), a mechanism-5 site contemptus is nominative masculine and agrees with sermo Dei, not with te. The English reads "lest, being despised, it accuse thee" — the word of God accusing, not the reader being accused for contempt. The easier reading here is the wrong one, which is the class the brief names as dominant.

### 0301D — petierunt for perierunt (0022) nuntiatur ei de Galilaeis, quod quasi juste ideo petierunt, quod non bene se praeparaverunt. Rendered "who as it were justly sought this for the cause that…". The same sentence continues et illos vere fuisse peccatores et juste periisse, and the next clause alios peccatores perituros, so perierunt is what the argument wants — one letter away. No marker, no [cj:]: the printed word is real, and internal corroboration proves an error exists, never whose it is (§e.4).

### 0302A — ex iota et ita (0022) The Greek for eighteen is ιη´, iota + eta. Migne prints ita, which is also the ordinary medieval Latin NAME of the letter η (Byzantine ita), so this is very possibly correct as printed. Rendered as printed, "by iota and ita", and flagged only so a plate reader can confirm the sort (§e.5).

### 0302B — Vinea Domini sabaoh (0022) A non-word: sabaoh for sabaoth. It sits inside an italic lemma, where a second italic cannot nest, and it is carried as printed. It could be Migne's dropped final sort or a Corpus Corporum loss; I do not know which and assert neither. No [sic:] (§e.6). The Vulgate's own exercituum at Is 5:7 is discussed at §c, span 46, and took no [var:].

### 0302B — the Is 9:2 proof-text, [var:] FIRED See §c, span 45. Three words replaced in a clause carrying its own address.

### 0302C — in Synagoga crebris docet (0022) crebris is an ablative plural adjective with no noun; the sense wants crebris [sermonibus] or an adverb. Rendered "teaches often in the Synagogue", which is the least that the printed words allow without supplying a noun. Logged (§e.7).

### 0303C — …curvata fuit, quae magis terrena quam coelestia noverat, operatur. (0022) Two finite verbs, the second stranded at the end of the sentence with no subject of its own. Rendered as printed, with operatur left where Migne sets it: "…was bent away from the uprightness of her mind, she who had known earthly things rather than heavenly, works." The conjecture that construes is noverat operari ("knew how to work earthly things rather than heavenly"). No marker (§e.8).

### 0304A — a paragraph with no terminal stop (0022) VERS. 16 ends …quod est filiam Abrahae a vinculo noxiae inclinationis erigi with no full stop. Pattern 8: none supplied. The English paragraph ends the same way, mid-air.

### 0304B — Deum de sua erectione glorificantem (0022), mechanism-5 checked The participle is accusative feminine and agrees with filiam Abrahae, id est Ecclesiam — so it is the Church that glorifies God for her raising up, not the rejoicing bystanders, who are the easier antecedent. Rendered accordingly.

### 0306C — Ὕδωρ, the only Greek in the work (0024) Ὕδωρ , aqua, inde hydropis, morbus aquosus subcutaneus. The Greek is reproduced verbatim, in Greek, with the space Migne sets before the comma. hydropis is set as a nominative where it is the genitive of hydrops; the English renders it "the dropsy", the one English word that keeps the ὕδωρ derivation visible (dropsy < hydropsy < ὕδρωψ), so that the etymology the sentence exists to make still works in the English. The choice is recorded rather than silent (§e.10).

### 0307A — the Rom 3:23 proof-text, [var:] FIRED; and (Rom. XXXV) gratia for gloria — see §c, span 96. The note's address names a chapter Romans does not have; reproduced verbatim, unmarked (§e.15).

### 0307A/B — the Ps 87:16 proof-text, [var:] FIRED confusus for conturbatus — see §c, span 100.

### 0307B — Honoratiori post invitato locum dat, qui … praeitur (0024), mechanism-5 site qui is the man who gives place — the over-confident earlier guest — and praeitur is passive, so he is outstripped by the nimbleness of those who followed Christ. The easier reading binds qui to the more honourable newcomer and makes the verb active; that reading is wrong on both the case and the voice. Rendered per the morphology.

### 0309B — domum intellectus accipiunt (0024) Quidam autem vocantur et contemnunt, qui domum intellectus accipiunt, sed opere non implent. Rendered "who receive the house of understanding". The parallel three lines later — qui acceptam intelligentiam opere adimplent — wants donum intellectus, "the gift of understanding," and domum/donum differ by one sort. Real word, so no [sic:]; not a negation or correlative, so no [cj:]. Logged (§e.11).

### 0309C — cognitione conjunctos (0024) ut et cognitione conjunctos, et divino praecepto diligamus. Rendered "those joined to us by acquaintance". The passage is about hating father, mother, wife and children while loving them, so the expected word is cognatione, "by kinship". One letter. No marker (§e.12).

### 0310B — piorum affectum (0024) legationem lacrymarum, bonorum operum, piorum affectum — three genitives, and the third is an accusative singular where the series requires affectuum. English has no exponent for the case, so the rendering "of holy affection" is unavoidable and the defect is invisible in the English; that is exactly why it is logged here (§e.13).

### 0310B — the 1 Cor 4:9 quotation, [var:] DECLINED Order and connectives only; see §c, span 137. The address (II Cor. IV) is wrong for the verse.

### 0310C — spiritualia sapientia (0025) ad condienda corda auditorum spiritualia sapientia: a neuter plural adjective against an ablative singular noun. Rendered "with spiritual wisdom"; Pattern 9 governs — English carries no exponent for the disagreement, there is nothing to emend and nothing to mark (§e.14).

### Mechanism-5 census — ALL FIVE CHUNKS, not just the first two The brief's warning that a stint's apparatus is silent about what it never looked for is answered here explicitly. Every site in this range where a pronoun, relative or participle could bind to either of two nouns was checked on case and gender rather than on sense: 0021 — ne contemptus te accuset (0301B, above) · a quo liberatur (0301B: quo = sermo Dei, the adversary of the lemma, not the judge) · qui cum in se nil haberet (0300B: qui = the Lord, not the devotion) · quem judex exactori … tradet (0301B: quem = the sinner). 0022 — quam tamen non ex toto exstirpandam (0303B: quam = the Synagogue) · Deum de sua erectione glorificantem (0304B, above) · quorum … uterque (0304A: the two peoples) · quae … vincula … erupit (0303D–0304A: the Church, not the soul). 0023 — quam in die resurrectionis glorificatam (0306A: quam = the Church, his body) · cui destructioni eorum … condoleo (0306A: the destruction, not the prophets). 0024 — qui … praeitur (0307B, above) · cum vix illum praeparatum … discutit (0310B: God is the subject of discutit, illum the object; the easier reading makes the man the examiner) · quos habemus ad malum suasores (0310A). 0025 — ne in eis germen oriretur (0310C: eis = the salted cities). No re-binding was found that the English had taken the easy way on; the two named above (0301B, 0307B) are the sites where the easy reading was available and refused.

### What I would most want read at the plate, in order

  1. 0302B sabaoh — a bare non-word, and the only one in the range. cols 301/302 = archive.org leaf n157 (leaf n = (column + 11) / 2).
  2. 0301D petierunt — same leaf as item 1, so one read settles both.
  3. 0309B domum and 0309C cognitione — adjacent columns, one leaf (n160), and both are one-sort differences from a word the context supplies.
  4. 0298B mundamini (leaf n155) and 0298D damnatorum (same leaf).
  5. 0302A ita — free, it is on the leaf of items 1–2; a confirmed ita closes the question and a printed eta opens a different one.

Preserved verbatim from cruces-0026.md. Where it states a [var:] threshold of its own, §0.1 governs.

Stint 6 of eight. Chunks 0026–0030, cols 0310C–0325C, CAPUT XV–XVIII — Luke's parable core: the lost sheep and the lost drachma, the prodigal son, the unjust steward, Dives and Lazarus, the ten lepers, the coming of the Son of man, the unjust judge, the Pharisee and the publican, the rich ruler, and blind Bartimaeus.

Sections in the order the launch brief requires: (a) the policy, before the findings · (b) the span count against the brief's 191 · (c) the lemma divergence list, all 191 spans · (d) this range's cruces by column · (e) the declined-oddity list, gathered in one place for the plate reader.

(a) THE POLICY THIS STINT APPLIED — stated before the findings so it can be argued with

a.1 Markers

  • [sic: …] fired: ZERO. Not one, in 5,985 Latin words. This is the brief's headline instruction and I have obeyed it literally: every suspect word in my range is in §(e), unmarked, with its column, and with no claim about whose the defect is. Where I have a conjecture I give it and I say plainly that a conjecture proves an error exists and never proves who made it. Two of my nineteen have on-page controls that read as decisive (amore at 0315B, praecepta at 0316B); I have written both down as controls that do not settle anything, because that is exactly the reasoning the brief records as running right 5 / wrong 9 on 9004.
  • [cj: …] fired: ZERO. Four sites in my range meet Pattern 18's shape — a real, well-formed Latin word whose faithful English misleads (negatur 0312A, amore 0315B, irritus 0316A, caecum 0325A). I declined all four. My reason is 18a's own test: the bar is whether the English asserts something false, and each of these produces a sentence that is odd or unconstruable rather than one that asserts a falsehood a reader would carry away. An odd sentence is visible to the reader; a false one is not. They are in §(e) instead.
  • [var: …] fired: TWO, both citable against sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt by book chapter:verse, both a whole word of the received text replaced or removed rather than clipped: 0321C In illa die (Lc 17:31 In illa hora) and 0322A qui habent tanquam non habentes (1 Cor 7:29 qui habent uxores, tamquam non habentes sint).
  • [d:], [ed:], [nt:], [cn:] fired: ZERO. No dittography, no lost text in the digitization, no note whose content is prose (all sixteen [n:] in my range are sigla or citations), no foot-of-page apparatus.

a.2 The line I drew on [var:], so the twenty declines below can be argued with

[var:] fires where a whole word or clause of the received text is replaced by different matter, or removed from inside the quotation, in a way that changes what the verse says. [var:] is declined, and the divergence logged here instead, where the difference is:

(i) a clip at either end of the quotation — Migne's citing practice throughout this recension, and the commonest single class in my range; (ii) an inflection, a single letter, or a word order — the class in which Corpus Corporum can manufacture a Vulgate divergence that a collation then dutifully "finds" (the judicabit→judicavit trap), and the class in which English frequently has no exponent at all, so the marker would be the only place the claim lived; (iii) an adaptation to the citing sentence — case, person, mood, or a finite verb made out of an infinitive so the verse can sit inside the gloss's own syntax.

⚑ The two markers I did fire are neither (i) nor (ii) nor (iii). hora→die is one substantive noun exchanged for another at the verse being glossed; the loss of uxores is a word taken out of the middle of a clause, which changes the object of habent from wives to possessions generally — and it is the second sense the gloss then argues from.

a.3 The inherited conventions (brief §1), and one exception

All six were applied from the first line, and each is audited in §(d) against the Latin rather than by impression. One exception was forced, and the Latin that forced it is at 0315D/0316A — see §(d), CHUNK 0027. It is usque ad inside Luke 16:16 itself.

a.4 Two places the English departs from what a reader might expect, declared up front

  1. Two non-words are carried into the English in italics, untranslated, and NOT marked — fideres @0321A and Testatamenti @0324D. Pattern 7/10 says a non-word is carried in italics; Pattern 12 would normally wrap it in [sic:]; this brief forbids the marker. I have followed the brief. The cost is that chunk 0028 has 47 italic spans against the Latin's 46, and chunk 0030 has 47 against 46. Both deltas are these two runs and nothing else. They are declared here so the delta is not read downstream as a structural defect, and so the merge can overrule me in one edit if it prefers the marker.
  2. Two supplied English words stand in square brackets — "he [Christ] says" @0323D and "lest [the Lord] be entreated on his behalf" @0325A. In both, Migne's Latin fixes the subject morphologically and English word order does not; without the bracket the English binds the clause to the wrong person, which is mechanism 5 committed by us rather than found.

a.5 What I did NOT report

Column-band arithmetic (my anchors run 0310D … 0314B, 0314C … 0317C, 0317D … 0321B, 0321C … 0322A, 0322B … 0325C, all monotone, no text broken across an anchor); Ibid. resolution; noteCount; Pattern 4 tails (this is lemma-and-gloss, not a florilegium); Pattern 9 items — of which my range has four and they are in §(e) as oddities only, not as defects. No <LACUNA>, no Migne ( sic ), no bracketed Forte occurs in my Latin.

(b) THE SPAN COUNT, RUN AGAINST THE BRIEF'S 191 — AND IT AGREES

data/briefs/9000-lemmata-0026.txt declares 191 spans for chunks 0026–0030, bands 0310C–0325C. I counted the … spans in my own five Latin files:

chunkitalic spans in the Latin

45 + 40 + 46 + 14 + 46 = 191. The totals agree. The file's first entry ([0310C] VERS. 4.-- Qui ex vobis,) is the first span of chunk 0026 and its last ([0325C] Et omnis plebs.) is the last span of chunk 0030, so the cut is aligned at both ends and no span has slid into or out of a neighbouring stint's file. The count was run; this is what it gave.

Column marks and notes were counted the same way and match the Latin twin verbatim, in order, in place — 15/13/15/3/14 anchors and 4/5/5/3/6 notes, checked mechanically against the Latin files, not by eye.

(c) THE LEMMA DIVERGENCE LIST — all 191 spans walked (7a″)

Every span was read word by word against sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt at the verse actually being glossed, which is the thing the pre-scan's ✓ does not do. Verdict vocabulary:

  • agrees — matches the glossed verse, allowing only Migne's orthography (Loth, -cunque).
  • clip — cut at one or both ends of the quotation, nothing else. Declined, class (i).
  • declined — a divergence inside the quotation, with the class and the reason given.
  • ⭐ VAR — [var:] fired.
  • formula — the abridgment's usque ad splice, not scripture.

⚑ Six of the ✓ marks in my brief file are false ✓ in the documented sense — the span occurs somewhere in the Clementine but not at its own verse: spans 1, 4, 32, 44, 49 and, most consequentially, span 132, where the pre-scan's ✓ on In illa die conceals the one clean [var:] in chunks 0026–0028.

(d) THIS RANGE'S CRUCES, BY COLUMN

CHUNK 0026 — cols 0310D–0314B

0311B · hic majoris misericordiae et majoris gaudii — MECHANISM 5, not resolved, rendered adverbially. The sentence is majus videtur impios justificare quam justos creare et conservare, quia etsi utrumque aequalis potentiae, hic majoris misericordiae et majoris gaudii. Read as a demonstrative pronoun, hic at its ordinary value is the LATTER of the two — creating and preserving the just — which reverses the clause the sentence has just made (majus videtur impios justificare). Read as the adverb, it commits to nothing. I rendered it adverbially: "here there is greater mercy and greater joy." Named because the contextually easy English ("this is of greater mercy", meaning the justification of the wicked) is a construal the printed pronoun does not license, and it is the reading a later reader will want to substitute.

0311C · Quod ubi inter homines claruit, domum evertit — MECHANISM 5, not resolved. Two candidate subjects for evertit: Quod (= verbum in carne, and the subject of claruit), and Dei sapientia, the main-clause subject two clauses back — who is also the woman of Lc 15:8, the one who lights the lamp and sweeps the house. There is no morphological discriminator: quod is neuter nominative, and evertit carries no gender. Rendered "And when this shone forth among men, it overturned the house", which leaves both open exactly as the Latin does.

0311C · evertit for the Vulgate's everrit. Lc 15:8 has everrit domum, "sweeps the house"; the gloss's own word is evertit, "overturns". ⚑ Almost certainly not a defect: the explanation attached to it is quia conscientias hominum de reatu suo perturbavit, which wants overturning and not sweeping. It is unitalicised gloss prose, not a lemma, so 7a″ does not reach it. Logged only because a mechanical collation will flag it.

0311D · Qui nec post culpam a Creatore periit — MECHANISM 5, resolved. Qui is masculine singular and there are two masculine candidates in the preceding clause, decimus electorum numerus and homo. Bound to numerus: it is the nearer, and the reason clause in the same sentence (eum sapientia Dei … ex lumine testae reparavit) belongs to the drachma-and-number image, not to the man. Rendered "And that number did not perish from its Creator".

0312A · a quo negatur. Declined oddity 1; see §(e). Rendered "by whom he is denied".

0312A · naturalibus solum ingenii … beneficium — a case with no host. Declined oddity 3.

0312B · THE PARAGRAPH ENDS WITH NO FULL STOP, AND SO DOES OURS. Haec est prodiga vita, quae amat fundere et spatiari in pompis exterioribus, relinquens eum qui sibi est interior — Migne prints no terminal mark. Pattern 8: never supply. The English stops where he stops. Not a defect to repair, and not a build error.

0313B · munite. Declined oddity 2. Rendered as the imperative it is — the two purpose clauses that follow (ut et opus ornetur … et cursus … praeparando) construe with it.

0313D · litterali sensu, contentus, — Migne's comma falls between the ablative and its adjective. Carried, mark for mark: "in the literal sense, content, working earthly things, awaiting earthly things." Pattern 8 forbids deleting a printed mark, and this is the class of comma that gets tidied away without trace.

0314A–0314B · convention 4 audited. Every gentes-family word in this chunk stands in a Judaei/gentes opposition — cur Deus laetetur in assumptione gentium against Israel outside the door; quia Jesus cum gentibus epularetur; de gentium salute; cum plenitudo gentium introierit; Sic de Judaeo et gentili; superstitiones gentilium against connubio verbi Dei. All are Gentiles. ⚑ natio does not occur anywhere in chunks 0026–0030 (0 instances), ethnicus does not occur, and no gentes in my range is generic — so nothing in this range is flattened by the convention and nothing was at risk.

CHUNK 0027 — cols 0314C–0317C

0314D · Et laudavit Dominus — Migne's capital, and the gloss reads it. Lc 16:8's dominus is the steward's master. Migne prints Dominus, and the gloss immediately builds on that reading: Si dispensator iniqui mammonae Domini voce laudatur. The English follows Migne and reads "the Lord". No marker: a capitalisation is not a textual variant, and firing on one would be a claim about compositor's practice.

0315B · Deum amore non posse — the sentence has no infinitive. Declined oddity 6. Rendered "he says that the lovers of money are not able God with love", which is not English and is meant not to be: 7a says that where the printed reading will not construe, render it as closely as the words allow and log the crux rather than substituting a word that works. The hole is left visible on the page.

0315D · recapitulatur singular against plural omnia. Declined oddity 8. ⚑ English "are recapitulated" has no exponent for the disagreement, so the rendering does conceal it; Pattern 12 would mark it and this brief forbids the marker, so it is recorded here instead.

0316A · sicut Dominus irritus est a Pharisaeis. Declined oddity 7. Rendered "as the Lord is made void by the Pharisees".

0316A · de terra genitu. Construed as an ablative of respect, "by birth from earth", which is what the printed form allows. No conjecture offered and none needed.

0316B · quia praecepta pretio redimere noluit. Declined oddity 9.

0316B · gentilibus — a fourth word on the convention-4 axis. Non enim gentilibus Christianae copulantur. The sense here is "pagans", not the typological Gentiles, and gentilis is to gentes what ethnicus is (brief §1.4). I rendered Gentiles, for consistency with the seven other sites in my range, and flag it so the merge can see the class rather than discover it as drift.

0316D · ⭐ injusti in requie IS NOT A DEFECT, AND IT READS AS ONE. The Gregory extract prints Credendum est, quod ante retributionem extremi judicii injusti in requie, etc. — as it stands, that the unjust are at rest before the judgment, which contradicts the whole column. It is not a dropped negative and it is not broken type: Gregory's sentence continues injusti in requie justos conspiciant, and the abridgment's etc. cuts the clause before its object. The fragment is a truncation artifact of the recension's own splicing. Rendered as the fragment it is ("the unjust in rest, etc."). No marker, no conjecture, and no plate read is owed on this column for this reason.** Recorded because a blind reader will otherwise find it and file it as a polarity defect.

0316D–0317A · A longe vident infideles — MECHANISM 5, resolved, and the easy reading is the wrong one. infideles and fideles are both nominative-equals-accusative plural, so the morphology does not decide the subject. The next clause does: fideles super se in requie attendunt puts the faithful above and at rest, so it is the unfaithful who see and the faithful who are seen. The tempting English — "the faithful see the unfaithful afar off" — would have inverted heaven and hell in a sentence about the day of judgment.

0317A · In sinum Abrahae … in quo … recipiuntur — MECHANISM 5, named, not decisive. quo may be sinus (masculine) or regnum (neuter); both are available and the sentence names both. Rendered so that English "which" follows "the kingdom of heaven", the nearer candidate and the idiomatic one. I do not claim it is settled.

0317C · Redemptoris praecepta qui ex mortuis surrexit — MECHANISM 5, resolved by number. surrexit is singular, so qui is the Redeemer and not the precepts, despite standing next to them. Rendered "the precepts of the Redeemer, who rose from the dead".

CHUNK 0028 — cols 0317D–0321B

0317D · non poterant non evenire — a double negative, carried in full. "which were not able not to come about in a world most full of hardships and errors." 7a: every particle the Latin prints appears in the English. The idiomatic English ("could not but happen") swallows one of them and I did not use it.

0317D · mortem moereri perpetuam. Declined oddity 10.

0318B · quae … est eradicata et … transplantata — MECHANISM 5, and the gender forbids the easy reading. The sentence is Evangelium crucis exprimitur, quae per fidem apostolorum de gente Judaeorum … est eradicata et in mare gentium transplantata. quae is FEMININE SINGULAR and therefore cannot be Evangelium, which is neuter. The available antecedents are crucis and the mulberry (morus, feminine — and the tree is what Lc 17:6 orders uprooted and transplanted into the sea). Rendered with a bare "which", which in English attaches to the whole phrase "the Gospel of the cross" and so preserves the Latin's own openness without asserting the binding the gender rules out.

0318B · sic diabolus, flore angelicae naturae et potestate rutilantis praevaricando terror inhorruit odore — will not construe. Declined oddity 11. rutilantis is a genitive participle with no host, and terror a nominative in a clause that already has a subject. Rendered as closely as the words allow and left visibly broken; no single word is nominable as the defect, so nothing is offered as a conjecture.

0318D · nec gratiam habet — MECHANISM 5 / idiom, and I got it wrong first. The phrase is Lc 17:9's own — Numquid gratiam habet servo illi — where gratiam habere means to thank someone. A first draft read "and has no thanks for it", which makes the master the ungratified party and turns the a-fortiori on its head. Corrected before this file was written, to "and gives him no thanks." Recorded because a crux that records a decision the text does not carry is the failure class John @0409B is named for, and the only defence is saying which way the edit ran.

0319B · unus … remanent. Declined oddity 12.

0319D · Fides quae ad agendas gratias inclinavit, econtra perfidia illos damnat — the first limb has no main verb. Migne's sentence is an anacoluthon. Rendered as the fragment it is: "The faith which inclined him to give thanks; on the contrary, faithlessness condemns those who are ungrateful for benefits received." No repair, no supplied verb.

0320B · Regnum Dei intra vos est, qui … regnat — MECHANISM 5, resolved by gender. qui is masculine and regnum is neuter, so the subject of regnat is the Lord, not the kingdom. The English keeps a singular "reigns" behind a plural "you", which is what excludes the second easy binding (to the addressees) that English word order would otherwise invite.

0320B · cum haeretici turbant Ecclesiam is the VERB turbare, not the noun turba. Rendered "trouble the Church". Named because the brief warns that a \bturba grep matches this and manufactured a false drift signal at John's merge. My range's two real turba nouns are at 0324B (turba major pauperum) and 0325B (phantasmatum turbas); both are crowd/crowds.

0320D · et quem fulguri … comparaverat — MECHANISM 5, and I got this one wrong first too. quem is masculine accusative and its antecedent is diem — Subitum adventus sui diem pluribus astruit exemplis, et quem fulguri … comparaverat, diebus Noe … aequiparat. A first draft read "and him whom he had compared to lightning", which made the compared term the Son rather than the day and then equated a person with the days of Noe. Corrected to "and that which he had compared to lightning".

0321A · ⭐ cum in his successionis, in illis naturae posita sint subsidia — MECHANISM 5, UNRESOLVED, and the most interesting site in my range. The sentence is Non connubia vel alimenta damnantur, cum in his successionis, in illis naturae posita sint subsidia. The argument plainly wants marriage → succession and food → nature. But taken at their ordinary values hic is the LATTER (alimenta) and ille the FORMER (connubia), which pairs food with succession and marriage with nature — the reverse. English "these"/"those" reproduce exactly the same mapping and exactly the same problem, so I rendered them straight and left the difficulty where Migne left it. ⛔ Do not "fix" this by exchanging the demonstratives. That is the silent repair 7a exists to forbid: it would produce smooth prose, leave no lexical trace, and pass every check we have.

0321A · fideres. Declined oddity 13 — a non-word, carried in italics and unmarked, and the first of my two span deltas (see §a.4).

CHUNK 0029 — cols 0321C–0322A

0321C · ⭐ [var:] FIRED at VERS. 31, In illa die for Lc 17:31's In illa hora. See span

  1. This is the one marker in my range that the pre-scan's ✓ actively hid.

0321D · statum vitae qua imbutus est — a feminine relative on a masculine noun. Declined oddity 14; Pattern 9, no English exponent, no emendation to make or undo.

0322A · quae tamen in unum molunt — MECHANISM 5, and there is NO morphological discriminator at all. Duae differentiae … Quae femineo genere designantur … ut feminae a viris, quae tamen in unum molunt. Both candidates — differentiae and feminae — are feminine plural. Decided by the parable: the two who grind together in Lc 17:35 are the two, i.e. the differences, not the women of the simile, and the qualification that follows (inquantum de suis negotiis Ecclesiae usibus deserviunt) describes the two classes. The English keeps "which" parallel to the earlier "Which are designated by the feminine gender", carrying the same subject forward. Named because it is decided on sense alone, which is the ground this brief distrusts, and a blind reader may reasonably disagree.

0322A · ⭐ [var:] FIRED at qui habent tanquam non habentes. See span 142.

0322A · Ubicunque fuerit Dominus corporaliter — the gloss substitutes Dominus for Lc 17:37's corpus. It is unitalicised gloss prose, not a lemma, so it is outside the inventory and outside 7a″; rendered as printed. Logged so that a later collation does not read the substitution as ours.

CHUNK 0030 — cols 0322B–0325C

0322B · quam nunc contra bonos quandiu hoc ipsum bonis expedit vel temporaliter aliquid possunt — a knotted period. The quandiu clause is interpolated between the relative and its verb. Rendered as closely as the words allow, with the interpolation kept where Migne has it rather than moved to the end where English would prefer it. No emendation.

0323A · unum foramen per quos hostes intrent. Declined oddity 15; Pattern 9.

0323A · Deus non faciet vindictam electorum ad se clamantium — unitalicised, therefore outside the inventory. Lc 18:7 reads Deus autem non faciet vindictam electorum suorum clamantium ad se die ac nocte. Migne's gloss compresses it and does not present it as a quotation (no italics), so no [var:] is owed and none is fired. Logged for completeness.

0323D · et non Deum vel Dei Filium confessus est dicit — a subject change across a gap Migne leaves unmarked. confessus est is the ruler; dicit is Christ, settled by the next sentence (Christus autem non renuit testimonium bonitatis, sed magistri absque Deo excludit bonitatem). An unmarked "he says" in English binds to the ruler and would have the ruler assert that a holy man is not good — the opposite of what he has just failed to confess. The English supplies "[Christ]" in square brackets, our word and visibly ours. Migne's missing punctuation between the two verbs is carried as printed.

0324A · contra naturam esse dives misericors — nominatives inside an accusative-and- infinitive. Declined oddity 16. English has no exponent, so the rendering ("a merciful rich man is against nature") conceals it; recorded here instead.

0324B–0325B · conventions 2 and 5 audited. turba → crowd (0324B, 0325B); multitudo does not occur in my range at all, so the pair cannot collapse here. daemonium → demon (0312A Cibus daemonum, 0314B cum daemone); diabolus → devil (0312B, 0312C, 0322A); satanas does not occur. mysterium → mystery (0323C, 0324C); sacramentum → sacrament (0313C ×2, 0315D). ⚑ Gloss-voice archaism: dicit/ait are says/said throughout, and every -eth form in my five chunks stands inside an italic span — the italic-membership test was run, not assumed.

0324D · Testatamenti. Declined oddity 17 — a non-word, carried in italics and unmarked, and the second of my two span deltas.

0325A · homo ad cognoscenda redit. Declined oddity 18.

0325A · significant caecum carnalium vitiorum. Declined oddity 19 — and note that the relative that follows is quae, neuter plural, binding to vitiorum, which is what shows that the thing signified must be a collective and not a person.

0325A · ne pro se exorari possit — MECHANISM 5 plus a voice, both preserved. exorari is passive, and the one entreated is the Lord: the parallel eight words earlier is ne Jesus ad illuminandum venire possit cor hominis. se is the blind man. Rendered "lest [the Lord] be entreated on his behalf", with the supplied subject bracketed. Making it active ("lest he obtain by entreaty for himself") would be 7a mechanism 3 — a passive turned active, leaving no lexical trace — and it is the rendering the sentence invites.

0325B · Respice, fides tua salvum te fecit. See span 189; a plate candidate, not a marker.

⚑ THE ONE CONVENTION EXCEPTION FORCED BY LUKE — 0316A, and the Latin that forced it

Convention 6 fixes the abridgment's splice formula usque ad as "as far as". My range has six occurrences of the formula proper — 0313A (AMBR.), 0315B (AUG.), 0316B (GREG.), 0316D (GREG.), 0323D (BEDA.), 0324A (AMBR.) — each italic and each following etc.,. All six are rendered "as far as". Two further, non-formula, unitalicised occurrences in ordinary prose are also rendered "as far as" and read naturally: in verbo usque ad peregrinationem nostram condescendens (0313A) and ire crescendo usque ad perfectiora (0319D).

The exception is span 66, at 0316A:

Quamvis dicam, Lex et prophetae usque ad Joannem, tamen non praedico legis destructionem.

This usque ad is Luke 16:16 itself — an italic scripture lemma quoted inside the gloss's own sentence, not a splice mark, and the very verse the chunk's VERS. 16 address (span 63, Lex et prophetae.) heads. Rendered "The law and the prophets until John." Applying convention 6 here would put the splice formula's English inside a lemma where nothing is spliced, which is the opposite of what the convention exists to mark. Mark's cruces record two such look-alikes on that book and decided them per occurrence; this is the same decision, and it is stated here so that a mechanical sweep does not "normalise" it back.

(e) DECLINED-ODDITY LIST — NINETEEN SUSPECT WORDS, NONE MARKED, ALL FOR THE PLATE

No [sic:] was fired anywhere in this range. Every entry below is rendered as printed (or carried in italics where it is not a word at all), and for every one of them I do not know whose the defect is and I do not claim to. Where an internal control exists I name it and say in the same breath that a control proves an error exists and never proves who made it.

Ordered by column, which is the order a plate reader will work in. Leaf calibration for this range, from the brief: patrologiaecursu0114mign, leaf n = (column + 11) / 2 → cols 0310–0325 sit on leaves n160–n168.

Two further plate candidates that are not single suspect words

  • 0325B Respice, fides tua salvum te fecit against Lc 18:42 te salvum fecit, with the received order printed at 0319D in the same stint's range as the on-page control (spans 189 and 111). A word-order transposition, unmarkable because our English has no exponent for it.
  • 0310D Imponit in humerum against Lc 15:5 in humeros suos, with the gloss's own humeris and humeri immediately after it (span 4).

What the next reader of this file should know in one line

Two [var:], zero [sic:], zero [cj:], nineteen declined oddities and eleven declined lemma divergences; 191 spans counted and agreeing with the brief; five mechanism-5 sites resolved, four named and left open, and two mis-bindings caught and repaired in my own draft (0318D gratiam habet, 0320D quem fulguri comparaverat) — both of which were fluent English that asserted the wrong thing.

Preserved verbatim from cruces-0031.md. Where it states a [var:] threshold of its own, §0.1 governs.

Capita XIX–XXII: the entry into Jerusalem, the temple disputes, the apocalyptic discourse, and the opening of the passion. 5,933 Latin words, 186 italic spans, 23 notes, 60 column anchors.

(a) THE POLICY I APPLIED — stated before the findings, so it can be argued with

1. No [sic:]. Not one, on any word, however good the internal argument. The launch brief's numbers govern: on John the 23 leaf-reads spent on fired markers produced zero corrections, and the nineteen declined oddities produced all seventeen. My attribution guess carries no evidential weight, and the brief's within-work table (cols 381–390 four of four Migne's; cols 399–415 five of five ours, ~200 columns apart in one book) says no prior may be inherited from a neighbouring range either. Every suspect word in this range is in §(e), named, unattributed, with the leaf that would settle it. Where a defect could be Migne's type or Corpus Corporum's transcription I name both and assert neither.

2. No [cj:] either. Pattern 18's bar is that the printed word must make the sentence say something the author is arguing against, and 18a scopes the extension to negations and correlatives. Three sites in my range came close and all three are declined in §(e), with the reason: @0332A utile cadaver (a coherent reading exists — the corpse they discarded was in fact of use, and the Gentiles took it up, which is the chapter's whole argument), @0335A illuc for illud (a pronoun slip, not a negation), and @0338D aut ne … aut (a genuine correlative disturbance, but I cannot name the true reading without the plate, and "where a reading is merely uncertain, it stays in the crux").

3. [var:] only for a divergent quoted CLAUSE I can cite against sources/vulgate/clementine-flat.txt by book chapter:verse. Five fired. I declined every one-word apparent divergence — aut/vel, enim added, converte/convertere, resurgent/resurgant, intraverit/intraret, non/nondum, dropped ergo — and every pure word-order or truncation case, following the peer stint that got this right this run. A single word is suspicion of our file; a semantically coherent divergent clause is evidence about Migne.

4. One [d:] fired, at @0326B (id est id est), under Pattern 11: an unmarked repetition would read as our build defect. ⚠ I do not know whose doubling it is — the marker's tooltip says Migne's plate, and I record here that the plate has not been read. It is also in §(e) as an oddity so the reader can settle it at leaf 168.

5. Inherited conventions §1 applied from the first chunk, not reconciled afterwards. Measured across my whole range, English against Latin:

axisLatin (chunks 31–35)Englishverdict
turba (noun only)6crowd 6exact 1:1
multitudo2multitude 2exact 1:1
daemonium0demon 0vacuous, nothing to flatten
diabolus / Satanas8devil 3 + Satan 5exact 1:1
mysterium5mystery 1 + mysteries 4exact 1:1
sacramentum9sacrament 3 + sacraments 6exact 1:1
natio3 (nationum)"nations" 31:1
gens sing. of the Jewish people2 (gente)"their own nation", "that whole nation"per convention 4
gentes plural19Gentiles 18 + nations 1see note below
italic usque ad3as far as 3exact 1:1, zero "down to"
gloss-voice archaism—-eth 3all three (cometh, receiveth, betrayeth) inside italic scripture spans; zero on a metalanguage verb
em-dashes00—

⚑ The one gentes rendered "nations" is @0335B populo illi per omnes gentes disperso — the plainly generic dispersion sense, which convention 4 permits. All eighteen others sit in the Judaei/gentes typology that runs through capita XIX–XX and take Gentiles.

⚑ -est second-person forms (knewest, reapest, givest, wishest, mayest) are Pattern 17, not archaism drift: each renders a Latin second-person singular in the gloss's own voice (noveras, metis, das, vis, resurgas), and Pattern 17 makes thou a number marker, not a reverence marker. They are not the class convention 1 forbids.

⚑ Both instrument warnings in the brief fired in my range and were caught. A bare \bturba grep here matches turbatam (@0335C, turbata faciem velant) and turbatis (@0333D, conscientia turbatis) — both the verb turbare, neither a crowd. A bare natio grep matches damnationem three times and damnationis once. My table above counts the noun forms only.

6. Negation fidelity (§4 / 7a). Every non, nec, neque, nisi, nullus, nihil, nequaquam, necdum the Latin prints stands in the English, including where carrying it leaves the sentence odd. Two notes: @0340A nonnunquam is one lexeme meaning "sometimes" and is rendered so — flagged here to pre-empt a particle count reading it as a lost negation; and @0332D Non enim potest quis esse Domini, nisi… is rendered "no one can be the Lord's, unless he has first renounced the world", which is the English exponent of non … quis, not a dropped particle.

7. Pattern 7 / Pattern 8 literalism, three visible costs, all deliberate. @0327B carries Migne's missing full stop (legis dicta Mna decem drachmas appendit), @0338C carries a second (poenitentiam agat praedixit et poenam), and @0338C ends a paragraph with no stop at all (et criminosa). Supplying the stops would have been a silent repair of exactly the class 9001 @0202D was nearly published with. @0329D carries a singular verb against a plural relative (humiliatur qui … indigent) rather than smoothing it to "they are humbled".

data/briefs/9000-lemmata-0031.txt declares 186 spans for chunks 0031–0035, bands 0325C–0339C.

(Cross-check on the English side: each chunk's paired runs equal its Latin's plus exactly the italic Latin I put inside my own [var:] markers — 0031 57+5=62, 0032 16+2=18, 0033 44+1=45, 0034 29+0=29, 0035 40+0=40.)

Marker parity, counted before writing each file and again after: column anchors 16 / 5 / 13 / 10 / 16 — verbatim, in order, in place; notes 8 / 0 / 7 / 3 / 5 — matching each frontmatter's noteCount, contents untranslated. node scripts/verify-english.mjs 9000 reports verify OK.

(c) THE LEMMA DIVERGENCE LIST — all 186 spans, in order

Five [var:] fired (rows 11, 14, 23, 65, 83). Every other divergence I saw is listed with the reason I declined it. The pre-scan column reproduces the brief's mark; ✓ is not clearance and ⚠ is not a divergence, and I walked every line either way.

11[0326B]Etsi cognovimus secundum carnem Christum, sed jam non novimus. Si enim mortuus est ex infirmitate, sed jam vivit ex virtute Dei [n: (II Cor. V, XIII)]⚠[var:] FIRED. A conflation of 2 Cor 5:16 + 13:4, and Migne's own note says so. Divergence marked: 13:4 reads crucifixus est ex infirmitate, Migne mortuus est; a lexical substitution in a quoted clause, and the gloss is expounding the cross (the sycomore). The 5:16 half also drops nunc — recorded inside the same marker, not separately marked.

14[0326B]Quare introisti ad gentes praeputium habentes [n: (Act. IX)]⚠[var:] FIRED. Vulg. Acts 11:3 ad viros praeputium habentes; Migne prints ad gentes. Material: the gloss is adduced precisely for the Jews' hatred of the Gentiles' salvation, so the divergence carries the argument. (Migne's printed ref Act. IX is wrong for Acts 11 — refDisplay convention: not corrected, not queried.)

22[0327A]Miserere mei, fili David,⚠DECLINED. Lc 18:38 / Mc 10:47 read Jesu fili David, miserere mei; Mt 15:22 Miserere mei, Domine fili David. Migne's is a word-order shuffle of a loose memory citation with no word added or lost that changes the sense. Reason for declining: word order alone in a two-word cry, and the gloss turns on nobilis not on the order.
23[0327A]Hosanna filio David: benedictus qui venit in nomine Domini rex Israel,⚠[var:] FIRED. A conflation matching no single verse: filio David is Matt 21:9, rex Israel is Jo 12:13, and Luke's own 19:38 — the verse under commentary — reads Benedictus qui venit rex in nomine Domini. Cited against both, and the marker names both witnesses.

34[0327D]Quae est spes nostra, aut gaudium, aut corona gloriae? Nonne vos ante Dominum Jesum [n: (I Thes. II)]⚠DECLINED. Vulg. 1 Thess 2:19 Quae est enim nostra spes aut gaudium … ante Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum estis in adventu ejus. Migne inverts nostra spes → spes nostra, drops enim, nostrum, Christum, and truncates. All order and truncation, the ordinary shape of a citation cut for the page. Declined.

65[0330A]Gloria in excelsis Deo. Et in terra pax hominibus,⚠[var:] FIRED. Vulg. Lc 2:14 Gloria in altissimis Deo; Migne prints in excelsis, which is the reading of Lc 19:38, the verse this gloss is expounding. That is the 7a″ shape exactly — the nativity verse conformed to the entry verse — and the gloss's whole point is the parallel between the two songs. Marked, citing Luke 2:14.

69[0330A]quia non cognoveris tempus.⚠DECLINED. Vulg. Lc 19:44 eo quod non cognoveris tempus visitationis tuae; Migne's italic span opens quia. The quia is the gloss's own connective folded into the span (et hoc ideo, quia non cognoveris tempus), so the only real divergence is a one-word connective and a truncation. Declined; rendered because thou hast not known the time.

88[0331C]Domine Deus virtutum, converte et respice de coelo, et vide et visita vineam istam [n: (Psal. LXXVII)]⚠DECLINED, and it is the closest call in the range. Vulg. Ps 79:15 Deus virtutum, convertere, respice de caelo; Migne prints Domine Deus virtutum, converte et respice. Two disturbances — an added vocative Domine, and converte (transitive) for convertere. Declined because each is a single word and the combined sense of the petition is unchanged; both are recorded here for the plate. (Migne's ref Psal. LXXVII is off by two; not corrected.)

117[0333B]Hi accipient damnationem.✓Truncation only: Lc 20:47 reads hi accipient damnationem majorem. The gloss's own next words supply the comparison in its own voice (sed istos majorem).
